# Sydney

This article is about the Australian city. For the greater metropolitan area, see [Greater Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Sydney). For the local government area, see [City of Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Sydney). For other uses, see [Sydney (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_\(disambiguation\)).

**Sydney**[^cite_note-7] is the [capital](181337.md) of the [state](4689096.md) of [New South Wales](21654.md) and the [largest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Australia_by_population) in [Australia](4689264.md).[^cite_note-8] The metropolis surrounds [Sydney Harbour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_Harbour) and extends about 80 kilometres (50 mi) from the [Pacific Ocean](23070.md) in the east to the [Blue Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Mountains_\(New_South_Wales\)) in the west, and about 80 kilometres (50 mi) from [Ku-ring-gai Chase National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ku-ring-gai_Chase_National_Park) and the [Hawkesbury River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawkesbury_River) in the north and north-west, to the [Royal National Park](101094.md) and [Macarthur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macarthur,_New_South_Wales) in the south and south-west.[^cite_note-9] Greater Sydney consists of 658 suburbs, spread across 33 local government areas. Residents of the city are colloquially known as "Sydneysiders".[^cite_note-10] The estimated population in June 2025 was 5,638,830,[^cite_note-ABSGCCSA-4] which is about 66% of the state's population.[^cite_note-ABSCapitalPop-11] The city's nicknames include the **Emerald City** and the **Harbour City**.[^cite_note-12]

There is evidence that [Aboriginal Australians](2912594.md) inhabited the [Greater Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Sydney) region at least 30,000 years ago, and [their engravings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_rock_engravings) and cultural sites are common. The [traditional custodians](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/traditional%20owner) of the land on which modern Sydney stands are the clans of the [Darug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darug), [Dharawal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharawal) and [Eora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eora).[^cite_note-Aboriginal_people_and_place23-13] During his [first Pacific voyage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_voyage_of_James_Cook) in 1770, [James Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cook) charted the eastern coast of Australia, making landfall at [Botany Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botany_Bay). In 1788, the [First Fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Fleet) of [convicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convicts_in_Australia), led by [Arthur Phillip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Phillip), founded Sydney as a British [penal colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penal_colony), the first European settlement in Australia.[^cite_note-manly.nsw\.gov.au-14] After [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II_in_Australia), Sydney experienced mass migration and by 2021 over 40 per cent of the population was born overseas. Foreign countries of birth with the greatest representation are mainland China, India, the United Kingdom, Vietnam and the Philippines.[^cite_note-Australian-Bureau-of-Statistics-15]

Despite being one of the most expensive cities in the world,[^cite_note-16][^cite_note-17] Sydney frequently ranks in the top ten [most liveable cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World's_most_liveable_cities).[^cite_note-mercer.com-18][^cite_note-19][^cite_note-20] It is classified as an [Alpha+ city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalization_and_World_Cities_Research_Network#Alpha) by the [Globalization and World Cities Research Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalization_and_World_Cities_Research_Network), indicating its influence in the region and throughout the world.[^cite_note-21][^cite_note-22] Ranked eleventh in the world for economic opportunity,[^cite_note-23] Sydney has an advanced [market economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_economy) with strengths in education, finance, manufacturing and [tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Sydney).[^cite_note-Tough_week_for_a_Sydney_success_story-24][^cite_note-Another_shot_at_making_city_a_finance_hub-25] The [University of Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Sydney) and the [University of New South Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_New_South_Wales) are ranked 18th and 19th in the world respectively.[^cite_note-26]

Sydney has hosted major international sporting events such as the [2000 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Summer_Olympics), the [2003 Rugby World Cup Final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_Rugby_World_Cup_final), and the [2023 FIFA Women's World Cup Final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_FIFA_Women's_World_Cup_final). The city is among the top fifteen most-visited cities worldwide,[^cite_note-27] with millions of tourists coming each year to see the city's landmarks.[^cite_note-Our_global_city-28] The city has over 1,000,000 hectares (2,500,000 acres) of [nature reserves and parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parks_in_Sydney),[^cite_note-29] and its [notable natural features](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecology_of_Sydney) include [Sydney Harbour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Jackson) and [Royal National Park](101094.md). The [Sydney Harbour Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_Harbour_Bridge) and the World Heritage-listed [Sydney Opera House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_Opera_House) are major tourist attractions. [Central Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_railway_station,_Sydney) is the hub of Sydney's suburban train, metro and light rail networks and longer-distance services. The main passenger airport serving the city is [Kingsford Smith Airport](266313.md), one of the world's oldest continually operating airports.[^cite_note-Sydney_Airport_overview-30]

## Toponymy

In 1788, Captain [Arthur Phillip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Phillip), the first governor of New South Wales, named the cove where the first British settlement was established [Sydney Cove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_Cove) after Home Secretary [Thomas Townshend, 1st Viscount Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Townshend,_1st_Viscount_Sydney).[^cite_note-Egan-1999-31] The cove was called *Warrane* by the Aboriginal inhabitants.[^cite_note-32] Phillip considered naming the settlement [Albion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albion), but this name was never officially used.[^cite_note-Egan-1999-31] By 1790 Phillip and other officials were regularly calling the township "Sydney".[^cite_note-33] Sydney was declared a city in 1842.[^cite_note-34]

The [Gadigal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadigal) (Cadigal) clan, whose territory stretches along the southern shore of [Port Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Jackson) from [South Head](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_Heads) to [Darling Harbour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darling_Harbour), are the traditional owners of the land on which the British settlement was initially established, and call their territory *Gadi* (*Cadi*). Aboriginal clan names within the Sydney region were often formed by adding the suffix "-gal" to a word denoting the name for their territory, a specific place in their territory, a food source, or totem. Greater Sydney covers the traditional lands of 28 known Aboriginal clans.[^cite_note-35]

## History

Main article: [History of Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Sydney)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Sydney).

### First inhabitants of the region



[Charcoal drawing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charcoal_\(art\)) of kangaroos in [Heathcote National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heathcote_National_Park)

The first people to inhabit the area now known as Sydney were [Aboriginal Australians](2912594.md) who had migrated from southeast Asia via northern Australia.[^cite_note-36] Flaked pebbles found in Western Sydney's gravel sediments might indicate human occupation from 45,000 to 50,000 years ago,[^cite_note-Attenbrow-2010c-37] while [radiocarbon dating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radiocarbon_dating) has shown evidence of human activity in the region from around 30,000 years ago.[^cite_note-Settlers'_history_rewritten2-38] Prior to the arrival of the British, there were 4,000 to 8,000 Aboriginal people in the greater Sydney region.[^cite_note-39][^cite_note-Aboriginal_people_and_place23-13]

The inhabitants subsisted on fishing, hunting, and gathering plants and shellfish. The diet of the coastal clans was more reliant on seafood whereas hinterland clans ate more forest animals and plants. The clans had distinctive equipment and weapons mostly made of stone, wood, plant materials, bone and shell. They also differed in their body decorations, hairstyles, songs and dances. Aboriginal clans had a rich ceremonial life, part of a belief system centring on ancestral, totemic and supernatural beings. People from different clans and language groups came together to participate in [initiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Initiation) and other ceremonies. These occasions fostered trade, marriages and clan alliances.[^cite_note-Attenbrow-2010a-40]

The earliest British settlers recorded the word '[Eora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eora)' as an Aboriginal term meaning either 'people' or 'from this place'.[^cite_note-41][^cite_note-Aboriginal_people_and_place23-13] The clans of the Sydney area occupied land with traditional boundaries. There is debate, however, about which group or nation these clans belonged to, and the extent of differences in language and rites. The major groups were the coastal Eora people, the Dharug (Darug) occupying the inland area from [Parramatta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parramatta) to the Blue Mountains, and the Dharawal people south of Botany Bay.[^cite_note-Aboriginal_people_and_place23-13] Darginung and Gundungurra languages were spoken on the fringes of the Sydney area.[^cite_note-Attenbrow-2010b-42]

The first meeting between Aboriginals and British explorers occurred on 29 April 1770 when Lieutenant James Cook landed at [Botany Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botany_Bay) (Kamay[^cite_note-45]) and encountered the [Gweagal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gweagal) clan.[^cite_note-46] Two Gweagal men opposed the landing party and one was shot and wounded.[^cite_note-47][^cite_note-48] Cook and his crew stayed at Botany Bay for a week, collecting water, timber, fodder and botanical specimens and exploring the surrounding area. Cook sought to establish relations with the Aboriginal population without success.[^cite_note-49]

### Convict town (1788–1840)



*The Founding of Australia, 26 January 1788, by Captain [Arthur Phillip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Phillip) R.N., Sydney Cove.* Painting by [Algernon Talmage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algernon_Talmage)



*Sydney Cove in 1788*, by [John Hunter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Hunter_\(Royal_Navy_officer\))

[Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain) had been sending convicts to its [American colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteen_Colonies) for most of the eighteenth century, and the loss of these colonies in 1783 was the impetus to establish a penal colony at Botany Bay. Proponents of colonisation also pointed to the strategic importance of a new base in the Asia-Pacific region and its potential to provide much-needed timber and flax for the navy.[^cite_note-50]

The [First Fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Fleet) of 11 ships under the command of Captain [Arthur Phillip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Phillip) arrived in Botany Bay in January 1788. It comprised more than a thousand settlers, including 736 convicts.[^cite_note-51] The fleet soon moved to the more suitable [Port Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Jackson) where a settlement was established at [Sydney Cove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_Cove) on 26 January 1788.[^cite_note-52] The colony of New South Wales was formally proclaimed by Governor Phillip on 7 February 1788. Sydney Cove offered a fresh water supply and a safe harbour, which Philip described as "the finest Harbour in the World ... Here a Thousand Sail of the Line may ride in the most perfect Security".[^cite_note-53]

The settlement was planned to be a self-sufficient [penal colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penal_colony) based on subsistence agriculture. Trade and shipbuilding were banned in order to keep the convicts isolated. However, the soil around the settlement proved poor and the first crops failed, leading to several years of hunger and strict rationing. The food crisis was relieved with the arrival of the [Second Fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Fleet_\(Australia\)) in mid-1790 and the [Third Fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Fleet_\(Australia\)) in 1791.[^cite_note-54] Former convicts received small grants of land, and government and private farms spread to the more fertile lands around [Parramatta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parramatta), [Windsor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Windsor,_New_South_Wales) and [Camden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camden,_New_South_Wales) on the [Cumberland Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumberland_Plain). By 1804, the colony was self-sufficient in food.[^cite_note-55]

A smallpox epidemic in April 1789 killed about half the region's Indigenous population.[^cite_note-Aboriginal_people_and_place23-13][^cite_note-56] In November 1790 [Bennelong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bennelong) led a group of survivors of the Sydney clans into the settlement, establishing a continuous presence of Aboriginal Australians in settled Sydney.[^cite_note-57]

Phillip had been given no instructions for urban development, but in July 1788 submitted a plan for the new town at [Sydney Cove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_Cove). It included a wide central avenue, a permanent Government House, law courts, hospital and other public buildings, but no provision for warehouses, shops, or other commercial buildings. Phillip promptly ignored his own plan, and unplanned development became a feature of Sydney's topography.[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59]



[Thomas Watling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Watling)'s *View of the Town of Sydney in the Colony of New South Wales*, 1799

After Phillip's departure in December 1792, the colony's military officers began acquiring land and importing consumer goods from visiting ships. Former convicts engaged in trade and opened small businesses. Soldiers and former convicts built houses on Crown land, with or without official permission, in what was now commonly called Sydney town. Governor [William Bligh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Bligh) (1806–08) imposed restrictions on commerce and ordered the demolition of buildings erected on Crown land, including some owned by past and serving military officers. The resulting conflict culminated in the [Rum Rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rum_Rebellion) of 1808, in which Bligh was deposed by the [New South Wales Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_South_Wales_Corps).[^cite_note-60][^cite_note-61]

Governor [Lachlan Macquarie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lachlan_Macquarie) (1810–1821) played a leading role in the development of Sydney and New South Wales, establishing a bank, a currency and a hospital. He employed a planner to design the street layout of Sydney and commissioned the construction of roads, wharves, churches, and public buildings. [Parramatta Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parramatta_Road), linking Sydney and Parramatta, was opened in 1811,[^cite_note-broomham3-62] and a road across the [Blue Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Mountains_\(New_South_Wales\)) was completed in 1815, opening the way for large-scale farming and grazing west of the [Great Dividing Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Dividing_Range).[^cite_note-Kingston-2006-63][^cite_note-64]

&#x20;Following the departure of Macquarie, official policy encouraged the emigration of free British settlers to New South Wales. Immigration to the colony increased from 900 free settlers in 1826–30 to 29,000 in 1836–40, many of whom settled in Sydney.[^cite_note-65][^cite_note-Fitzgerald-2011-66] By the 1840s, Sydney exhibited a geographic divide between poor and working-class residents living west of the [Tank Stream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tank_Stream) in areas such as [The Rocks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Rocks,_New_South_Wales), and the more affluent residents living to its east.[^cite_note-Fitzgerald-2011-66] Free settlers, free-born residents and former convicts now represented the vast majority of the population of Sydney, leading to increasing public agitation for responsible government and an end to transportation. Transportation to New South Wales ceased in 1840.[^cite_note-City-of-Sydney-2020-67]



The [Castle Hill convict rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castle_Hill_convict_rebellion) of 1804

#### Conflict on the Cumberland Plain

In 1804, Irish convicts led around 300 rebels in the [Castle Hill Rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castle_Hill_convict_rebellion), an attempt to march on Sydney, commandeer a ship, and sail to freedom.[^cite_note-68] Poorly armed, and with their leader Philip Cunningham captured, the main body of insurgents were routed by about 100 troops and volunteers at [Rouse Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rouse_Hill,_New_South_Wales). At least 39 convicts were killed in the uprising and subsequent executions.[^cite_note-69][^cite_note-whitaker22-70]

As the colony spread to the more fertile lands around the [Hawkesbury River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawkesbury_River), north-west of Sydney, conflict between the settlers and the [Darug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darug) people intensified, reaching a peak from 1794 to 1810. Bands of Darug people, led by [Pemulwuy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pemulwuy) and later by his son [Tedbury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tedbury), burned crops, killed livestock and raided settler stores in a pattern of resistance that was to be repeated as the [colonial frontier expanded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_frontier_wars). A military garrison was established on the Hawkesbury in 1795. The death toll from 1794 to 1800 was 26 settlers and up to 200 Darug.[^cite_note-71][^cite_note-72]

Conflict again erupted from 1814 to 1816 with the expansion of the colony into Dharawal country in the Nepean region south-west of Sydney. Following the deaths of several settlers, Governor Macquarie dispatched three military detachments into Dharawal lands, culminating in the [Appin massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appin_Massacre) (April 1816) in which at least 14 Aboriginal people were killed.[^cite_note-73][^cite_note-74]

### Colonial city (1841–1900)

&#x20;The [New South Wales Legislative Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_South_Wales_Legislative_Council) became a semi-elected body in 1842. Sydney was declared a city the same year, and a governing council established, elected on a restrictive property franchise.[^cite_note-City-of-Sydney-2020-67]



Aerial illustration of Sydney, 1888

The discovery of gold in New South Wales and Victoria in 1851 initially caused economic disruption as men moved to the goldfields. Melbourne soon overtook Sydney as Australia's largest city, leading to an enduring rivalry between the two. However, increased immigration from overseas and wealth from gold exports increased demand for housing, consumer goods, services and urban amenities.[^cite_note-Goodman-2013-75] The New South Wales government also stimulated growth by investing heavily in railways, trams, roads, ports, telegraph, schools and urban services.[^cite_note-76] The population of Sydney and its suburbs grew from 95,600 in 1861 to 386,900 in 1891.[^cite_note-77] The city developed many of its characteristic features. The growing population packed into rows of terrace houses in narrow streets. New public buildings of sandstone abounded, including at the [University of Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Sydney) (1854–61),[^cite_note-78] the [Australian Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Museum) (1858–66),[^cite_note-79] the Town Hall (1868–88),[^cite_note-80] and the [General Post Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Post_Office,_Sydney) (1866–92).[^cite_note-81] Elaborate [coffee palaces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coffee_palace) and hotels were erected.[^cite_note-Noyce-2012-82] Daylight bathing at Sydney's beaches was banned, but segregated bathing at designated ocean baths was popular.[^cite_note-83]

Drought, the winding down of public works and a financial crisis led to economic depression in Sydney throughout most of the 1890s. Meanwhile, the Sydney-based premier of New South Wales, [George Reid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Reid), became a key figure in the process of federation.[^cite_note-84]

### State capital (1901–present)



A [tramcar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tramcar) on George Street in 1920. Sydney once had one of the largest [tram networks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trams_in_Sydney) in the British Empire.

When the six colonies federated on 1 January 1901, Sydney became the capital of the State of New South Wales. The spread of [bubonic plague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bubonic_plague) in 1900 prompted the state government to modernise the wharves and demolish inner-city slums. The outbreak of the First World War in 1914 saw more Sydney males volunteer for the armed forces than the Commonwealth authorities could process, and helped reduce unemployment. Those returning from the war in 1918 were promised "homes fit for heroes" in new suburbs such as Daceyville and Matraville. "Garden suburbs" and mixed industrial and residential developments also grew along the rail and tram corridors.[^cite_note-Fitzgerald-2011-66] The population reached one million in 1926, after Sydney had regained its position as the most populous city in Australia.[^cite_note-85] The government created jobs with massive public projects such as the electrification of the [Sydney rail network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railways_in_Sydney) and building the Sydney Harbour Bridge.[^cite_note-86]



Sydney Harbour Bridge opening day, 19 March 1932



The Sydney skyline at night in 1938.

Sydney was more severely affected by the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression) of the 1930s than regional New South Wales or Melbourne.[^cite_note-87] New building almost came to a standstill, and by 1933 the unemployment rate for male workers was 28 per cent, but over 40 per cent in working class areas such as Alexandria and Redfern. Many families were evicted from their homes and shanty towns grew along coastal Sydney and Botany Bay, the largest being "Happy Valley" at [La Perouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Perouse,_New_South_Wales).[^cite_note-88] The Depression also exacerbated political divisions. In March 1932, when populist Labor premier [Jack Lang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_Lang_\(Australian_politician\)) attempted to open the Sydney Harbour Bridge he was upstaged by [Francis de Groot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_de_Groot) of the far-right [New Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Guard), who slashed the ribbon with a sabre.[^cite_note-89]

In January 1938, Sydney celebrated the [Empire Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_Games) and the sesquicentenary of European settlement in Australia. One journalist wrote, "Golden beaches. Sun tanned men and maidens...Red-roofed villas terraced above the blue waters of the harbour...Even [Melbourne](17306237.md) seems like some grey and stately city of Northern Europe compared with Sydney's sub-tropical splendours.". A congress of the "Aborigines of Australia" declared 26 January "A [Day of Mourning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Day_of_Mourning_\(Australia\))" for "the whiteman's seizure of our country."[^cite_note-90]

With the outbreak of [Second World War](32927.md) in 1939, Sydney experienced a surge in industrial development. Unemployment virtually disappeared and women moved into jobs previously typically reserved for males. Sydney was attacked by [Japanese submarines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attack_on_Sydney_Harbour) in May and June 1942 with 21 killed. Households built [air raid](323167.md) shelters and performed drills.[^cite_note-91] [Military establishments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_engineering) in response to [World War II in Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_Australia_during_World_War_II) included the [Garden Island Tunnel System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garden_Island_Tunnel_System), the only [tunnel warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tunnel_warfare) complex in Sydney, and the heritage-listed military [fortification](204118.md) systems [Bradleys Head Fortification Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bradleys_Head_Fortification_Complex) and [Middle Head Fortifications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Head_Fortifications), which were part of a total [defence system for Sydney Harbour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_Harbour_defences).[^cite_note-92]

A post-war immigration and [baby boom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baby_boom) saw a rapid increase in Sydney's population and the spread of low-density housing in suburbs throughout the Cumberland Plain. Immigrants—mostly from Britain and continental Europe—and their children accounted for over three-quarters of Sydney's population growth between 1947 and 1971.[^cite_note-93] The newly created Cumberland County Council oversaw low-density residential developments, the largest at [Green Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Valley,_New_South_Wales) and [Mount Druitt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Druitt). Older residential centres such as Parramatta, [Bankstown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bankstown) and [Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool,_New_South_Wales) became suburbs of the metropolis.[^cite_note-94] Manufacturing, protected by high tariffs, employed over a third of the workforce from 1945 to the 1960s. However, as the long post-war economic boom progressed, retail and other service industries became the main source of new jobs.[^cite_note-95]

An estimated one million onlookers, most of the city's population, watched [Queen Elizabeth II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_II) land in 1954 at Farm Cove where Captain Phillip had raised the Union Jack 165 years earlier, commencing her [Australian Royal Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_visits_to_Australia). It was the first time a reigning monarch stepped onto Australian soil.[^cite_note-96]

Increasing high-rise development in Sydney and the expansion of suburbs beyond the "green belt" envisaged by the planners of the 1950s resulted in community protests. In the early 1970s, trade unions and resident action groups imposed [green bans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_ban) on development projects in historic areas such as The Rocks. Federal, State and local governments introduced heritage and environmental legislation.[^cite_note-Fitzgerald-2011-66] The Sydney Opera House was also controversial for its cost and disputes between architect [Jørn Utzon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%B8rn_Utzon) and government officials. However, soon after it opened in 1973 it became a major tourist attraction and symbol of the city.[^cite_note-97] The progressive reduction in tariff protection from 1974 began the transformation of Sydney from a manufacturing centre to a "world city".[^cite_note-98] From the 1980s, [overseas immigration](1100670.md) grew rapidly, with Asia, the Middle East and Africa becoming major sources. By 2021, the population of Sydney was over 5.2 million, with 40% of the population born overseas. China and India overtook England as the largest source countries for overseas-born residents.[^cite_note-99]

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Sydney)

### Topography



Sydney lies on a [submergent coastline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Submergent_coastline) where the ocean level has risen to flood deep [rias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ria).

Sydney is a coastal basin with the [Tasman Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tasman_Sea) to the east, the [Blue Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Mountains_\(New_South_Wales\)) to the west, the Hawkesbury River to the north, and the [Woronora Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woronora_Plateau) to the south.

Sydney spans two geographic regions. The [Cumberland Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumberland_Plain) lies to the south and west of the Harbour and is relatively flat. The [Hornsby Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hornsby_Plateau) is located to the north and is dissected by steep valleys. The flat areas of the south were the first to be developed; it was not until the construction of the Sydney Harbour Bridge that the northern reaches became more heavily populated. [Seventy surf beaches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beaches_in_Sydney) can be found along its coastline, with Bondi Beach being the most famous.

The [Nepean River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepean_River) wraps around the western edge of the city and becomes the Hawkesbury River before reaching [Broken Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broken_Bay). Most of Sydney's water storages can be found on tributaries of the Nepean River. The [Parramatta River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parramatta_River) is mostly industrial and drains a large area of Sydney's western suburbs into Port Jackson. The southern parts of the city are drained by the [Georges River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georges_River) and the [Cooks River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooks_River) into Botany Bay.

There is no single definition of the boundaries of Sydney. The Australian Statistical Geography Standard definition of Greater Sydney covers 12,369 square kilometres (4,776 square miles), and includes the local government areas of [Central Coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Coast_Council_\(New_South_Wales\)) in the north, [Hawkesbury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Hawkesbury) in the north-west, [Blue Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Blue_Mountains) in the west, [Sutherland Shire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sutherland_Shire) in the south, and [Wollondilly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wollondilly_Shire) in the south-west.[^cite_note-AU_Stats-2022-100] The local government area of the [City of Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Sydney) covers about 26 square kilometres from [Garden island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garden_Island_\(New_South_Wales\)) in the east to Bicentennial Park in the west, and south to the suburbs of Alexandria and [Rosebery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosebery,_New_South_Wales).[^cite_note-101]

### Geology

Further information: [Sydney sandstone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_sandstone) and [Geography of Sydney § Geology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Sydney#Geology)



Almost all of the exposed rocks around Sydney are [Sydney sandstone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_sandstone).

Sydney is made up of mostly [Triassic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triassic) rock with some recent [igneous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igneous) dykes and [volcanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volcanic) necks (typically found in the [Prospect dolerite intrusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prospect_dolerite_intrusion), west of Sydney).[^cite_note-102] The [Sydney Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_Basin) was formed in the [early Triassic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Triassic) period.[^cite_note-Sydney_Basin-103] The sand that was to become the sandstone of today was laid down between 360 and 200 million years ago. The sandstone has traditionally been interpreted as the product of a large [braided river](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Braided_river) system, comparable in scale and depositional style to the modern [Brahmaputra River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmaputra_River) in [Bangladesh](3454.md).[^cite_note-104] The sandstone has [shale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shale) lenses and fossil riverbeds.[^cite_note-Sydney_Basin-103]

The [Sydney Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_Basin) bioregion includes coastal features of cliffs, beaches, and estuaries. Deep river valleys known as [rias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ria) were carved during the Triassic period in the Hawkesbury sandstone of the coastal region. The rising sea level between 18,000 and 6,000 years ago flooded the rias to form estuaries and deep harbours.[^cite_note-Sydney_Basin-103] [Port Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Jackson), better known as Sydney Harbour, is one such ria.[^cite_note-105] Sydney features two major soil types: [sandy soils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sand) (which originate from the Hawkesbury sandstone) and [clay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clay) (which are from shales and [volcanic rocks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volcanic_rock)), though some soils may be a mixture of the two.[^cite_note-106]

Directly overlying the older Hawkesbury sandstone is the [Wianamatta shale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wianamatta_shale), a geological feature found in western Sydney that was deposited in connection with a large [river delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_delta) during the [Middle Triassic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Triassic). The Wianamatta shale generally comprises fine grained [sedimentary rocks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sedimentary_rock) such as shales, [mudstones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mudstone), [ironstones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ironstone), [siltstones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siltstone) and [laminites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laminite), with less common sandstone units.[^cite_note-basinguide-107] The Wianamatta Group is made up of [Bringelly Shale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bringelly_Shale), [Minchinbury Sandstone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minchinbury_Sandstone) and [Ashfield Shale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashfield_Shale).[^cite_note-108] Sydney is also crossed by several igneous intrusions, the most prominent of which is the [Great Sydney Dyke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Sydney_Dyke), a [Jurassic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurassic) dolerite intrusion extending for at least 9 kilometres (5.6 mi) across the inner city from the [Glebe Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glebe_Island) area towards the eastern coastline.[^cite_note-109] The [continental shelf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_shelf) of [Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia_\(continent\)) is only 25.9 km (16.1 mi) away from the coast of Sydney, and that is where the [Tasman Abyssal Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tasman_Abyssal_Plain) lies.[^cite_note-110][^cite_note-111]

### Ecology

Further information: [Ecology of Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecology_of_Sydney)



Typical [grassy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grassland) woodland in the Sydney metropolitan area

The most prevalent [plant communities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vegetation) in the Sydney region are grassy woodlands (i.e. [savannas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savanna))[^cite_note-112] and some pockets of dry [sclerophyll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sclerophyll) forests,[^cite_note-113] which consist of [eucalyptus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eucalyptus) trees, [casuarinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casuarina), [melaleucas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melaleuca), [corymbias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corymbia) and [angophoras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angophora), with shrubs (typically [wattles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acacia), [callistemons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Callistemon), [grevilleas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grevillea) and [banksias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banksia)), and a semi-continuous grass in the [understory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Understory).[^cite_note-114] The plants in this community tend to have rough, spiky leaves due to low [soil fertility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soil_fertility). Sydney also features a few areas of wet sclerophyll forests in the wetter, elevated areas in the [north](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hills_District,_New_South_Wales) and [northeast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Shore_\(Sydney\)). These forests are defined by straight, tall tree [canopies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canopy_\(biology\)) with a moist understory of soft-leaved shrubs, [tree ferns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tree_ferns) and herbs.[^cite_note-115]

The predominant vegetation community in Sydney is the [Cumberland Plain Woodland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumberland_Plain_Woodland) in [Western Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Sydney) ([Cumberland Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumberland_Plain)),[^cite_note-116] followed by the [Sydney Turpentine-Ironbark Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_Turpentine-Ironbark_Forest) in the Inner West and [Northern Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Sydney),[^cite_note-environment-117] the [Eastern Suburbs Banksia Scrub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Suburbs_Banksia_Scrub) in the coastline and the [Blue Gum High Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Gum_High_Forest) scantily present in the North Shore – all of which are critically endangered.[^cite_note-Sydney_Blue_Gum_High_Forest_of_the_Sydney_Basin-118][^cite_note-119] The city also includes the [Sydney Sandstone Ridgetop Woodland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_Sandstone_Ridgetop_Woodland) found in [Ku-ring-gai Chase National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ku-ring-gai_Chase_National_Park) on the [Hornsby Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hornsby_Plateau) to the north.[^cite_note-ryde-120]

According to the [Senseable City Lab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senseable_City_Lab) at the [Massachusetts Institute of Technology](18879.md) (MIT), which measures street-level [urban greenery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_forestry) using the Green View Index (GVI), Sydney recorded a GVI of 25.9%. Among the cities assessed, Sydney ranked fourth globally for visible street tree cover, behind [Tampa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tampa) (36.1%), [Singapore](27318.md) (29.3%) and [Oslo](22309.md) (28.8%), and was equal with [Vancouver](32706.md) (25.9%).[^cite_note-121][^cite_note-122]

Sydney is home to dozens of [bird](160118.md) species,[^cite_note-123] which commonly include the [Australian raven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_raven), [Australian magpie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_magpie), [crested pigeon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crested_pigeon), [noisy miner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noisy_miner) and the [pied currawong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pied_currawong). [Introduced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Introduced_species) bird species ubiquitously found in Sydney are the [common myna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_myna), [common starling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_starling), [house sparrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_sparrow) and the [spotted dove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spotted_dove).[^cite_note-124] [Reptile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reptiles_of_Australia) species are also numerous and predominantly include [skinks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skink).[^cite_note-125][^cite_note-126] Sydney has a few [mammal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mammals_of_Australia) and [spider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiders_of_Australia) species, such as the [grey-headed flying fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grey-headed_flying_fox) and the [Sydney funnel-web](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_funnel-web), respectively,[^cite_note-127][^cite_note-Whyte-2017-128] and has a huge diversity of [marine species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_life) inhabiting its harbour and beaches.[^cite_note-129]

### Climate

Main articles: [Climate of Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Sydney), [Climate history of Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_history_of_Sydney), and [Severe weather events in Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Severe_weather_events_in_Sydney)



A summer storm over Sydney Harbour



Lightning is a frequent occurrence in the spring and summer months in Sydney.

Under the [Köppen–Geiger classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification), Sydney has a [humid subtropical climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_subtropical_climate) (*Cfa*)[^cite_note-130] with "warm, sometimes hot" summers and "generally mild",[^cite_note-131][^cite_note-Bureau-of-Meteorology-Climate-water-132][^cite_note-133] to "cool" winters.[^cite_note-134] The [El Niño–Southern Oscillation](317311.md), the [Indian Ocean Dipole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Ocean_Dipole) and the [Southern Annular Mode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Annular_Mode)[^cite_note-ABC-News-2018-135][^cite_note-136] play an important role in determining Sydney's weather patterns: drought and [bushfire](20827796.md) on the one hand, and storms and flooding on the other, associated with the opposite [phases of the oscillation in Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effects_of_the_El_Ni%C3%B1o%E2%80%93Southern_Oscillation_in_Australia). The weather is [moderated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_breeze) by proximity to the ocean, and more extreme temperatures are recorded in the inland western suburbs because Sydney CBD is more affected by the [oceanic climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_climate) drivers than the western suburbs.[^cite_note-MDPI-137][^cite_note-Bureau-of-Meteorology-Climate-stats-138]

At Sydney's primary weather station at [Observatory Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_Observatory), extreme temperatures have ranged from 45.8 °C (114.4 °F) on 18 [January 2013](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angry_Summer) to 2.1 °C (35.8 °F) on 22 June 1932.[^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140][^cite_note-141] An average of 14.9 days a year have temperatures at or above 30 °C (86 °F) in the central business district (CBD).[^cite_note-Bureau-of-Meteorology-Climate-stats-138] In contrast, the metropolitan area averages between 35 and 65 days, depending on the suburb.[^cite_note-142] The hottest day in the metropolitan area occurred in [Penrith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penrith,_New_South_Wales) on 4 January 2020, where a high of 48.9 °C (120.0 °F) was recorded.[^cite_note-143] The average annual temperature of the sea ranges from 18.5 °C (65.3 °F) in September to 23.7 °C (74.7 °F) in February.[^cite_note-144] Sydney has an average of 7.2 hours of sunshine per day[^cite_note-145] and 109.5 clear days annually.[^cite_note-Bureau-of-Meteorology-Obs-Hill-6] Due to the inland location, [frost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frost) is recorded early in the morning in [Western Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Sydney) a few times in winter. Autumn and spring are the transitional seasons, with spring showing a larger temperature variation than autumn.[^cite_note-146]

Sydney experiences an [urban heat island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_heat_island) effect.[^cite_note-UrbanHeatIsland-147] This makes certain parts of the city more vulnerable to extreme heat, including coastal suburbs.[^cite_note-UrbanHeatIsland-147][^cite_note-148] In late spring and summer, temperatures over 35 °C (95 °F) are not uncommon,[^cite_note-149] though hot, dry conditions are usually ended by a [southerly buster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southerly_buster),[^cite_note-150] a powerful southerly that brings [gale](320909.md) winds and a rapid fall in temperature.[^cite_note-151] Since Sydney is downwind of the [Great Dividing Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Dividing_Range), it occasionally experiences dry, westerly [foehn winds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_foehn_winds) typically in winter and early spring (which are the reason for its warm maximum temperatures).[^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154] Westerly winds are intense when the [Roaring Forties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roaring_Forties) (or the [Southern Annular Mode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Annular_Mode)) shift towards southeastern Australia,[^cite_note-155] where they may damage homes and [affect flights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flight_cancellation_and_delay), in addition to [making the temperature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apparent_temperature) seem [colder than it actually is](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_chill).[^cite_note-156][^cite_note-157]

Rainfall has a moderate to low variability and has historically been fairly uniform throughout the year, although in recent years it has been more summer-dominant and erratic.[^cite_note-158][^cite_note-159][^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161] Precipitation is usually higher in summer through to autumn,[^cite_note-Bureau-of-Meteorology-Climate-water-132] and lower in late winter to early spring.[^cite_note-ABC-News-2018-135][^cite_note-162][^cite_note-Bureau-of-Meteorology-Climate-stats-138][^cite_note-163] In late autumn and winter, [east coast lows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_east_coast_low) may bring large amounts of rainfall, especially in the CBD.[^cite_note-164] In the warm season [black nor'easters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_nor'easter) are usually the cause of heavy rain events, though other forms of [low-pressure areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low-pressure_area), including remnants of [ex-cyclones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-tropical_cyclone), may also bring heavy deluge and afternoon thunderstorms.[^cite_note-165][^cite_note-166] 'Snow' was last alleged in 1836, more than likely a fall of [graupel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graupel), or soft hail; and in July 2008 the [Upper North Shore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Shore_\(Sydney\)) saw a fall of graupel that was mistaken by many for 'snow'.[^cite_note-167] In 2009, dry conditions brought a severe [dust storm towards the city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Australian_dust_storm).[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169]
